2020 Singaporean general election

General elections were held in [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore "Singapore") on 10 July 2020 to elect members of [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Singapore "Parliament of Singapore"). They were the fifteenth general elections since the introduction of [self-government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-governance_of_Singapore "Self-governance of Singapore") in 1959 and the thirteenth since [independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Singapore_Agreement_1965 "Independence of Singapore Agreement 1965") in 1965. Parliament was [dissolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_parliament "Dissolution of parliament") and the [general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_election "General election") called by [President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Singapore "President of Singapore") [Halimah Yacob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halimah_Yacob "Halimah Yacob") on 23 June, on the advice of [Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Singapore "Prime Minister of Singapore") [Lee Hsien Loong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Hsien_Loong "Lee Hsien Loong"). The number of elected seats was increased to 93 from the 89 in the [previous election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Singaporean_general_election "2015 Singaporean general election"). The 2020 election was the last general election under Lee's tenure as Prime Minister prior to the handover of his successor, [Lawrence Wong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Wong "Lawrence Wong"), in May 2024.

The election was significantly influenced by the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic "COVID-19 pandemic"), which became a central issue in the campaign. The governing [People's Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Action_Party "People's Action Party") (PAP) emphasised its [management of the crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Singapore "COVID-19 pandemic in Singapore"), including securing supplies of face masks and increasing [tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_testing "COVID-19 testing"). In contrast, opposition parties criticised the government's handling of the pandemic, particularly the high number of COVID-19 cases in foreign worker dormitories and the [Ministry of Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Health_(Singapore) "Ministry of Health (Singapore)")'s reversal of its guidance on mask usage. Beyond the pandemic, other key issues that emerged during the election included the [cost of living](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cost_of_living "Cost of living"), [housing affordability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_housing_in_Singapore "Public housing in Singapore"), [population growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Singapore "Demographics of Singapore") and [immigration policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Singapore "Immigration to Singapore"). The election also featured constituency political broadcasts, a televised programme hosted by [Mediacorp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediacorp "Mediacorp"), which replaced physical rallies that were suspended as part of [social distancing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_distancing "Social distancing") measures.

For only the second consecutive election since [independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Singapore_Agreement_1965 "Independence of Singapore Agreement 1965"), no seats were elected via a [walkover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walkover "Walkover") on nomination day. On polling day, the ruling PAP secured its 15th consecutive term in government since [1959](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1959_Singaporean_general_election "1959 Singaporean general election"), winning 83 of the 93 elected seats and retaining its [supermajority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermajority "Supermajority"). The [Workers' Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers'_Party_(Singapore) "Workers' Party (Singapore)") (WP) captured the remaining 10 with a record-best 50.49% of the contested vote across the six constituencies it contested against the PAP, making it the first time in a general election where the PAP lost the combined popular vote in constituencies contested by an opposition party. Despite the challenges posed by the pandemic, the election also recorded the highest voter turnout since [1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Singaporean_general_election "1997 Singaporean general election"), and it was the first election in which more than 1 million votes were not cast for the PAP (invalid or otherwise for the opposition).[&#8202;](#ref-back-2-0)[\[a\]](#refnote-2)

## Background

According to Article 65(4) of the [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Singapore "Constitution of Singapore"), the maximum term of any given [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Singapore "Parliament of Singapore") is five years from the date of its first sitting following a general election, after which it is dissolved by operation of law. However, the [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Singapore "Prime Minister of Singapore") may advise the [President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Singapore "President of Singapore") to dissolve Parliament at any time during the five-year period.[&#8202;](#ref-back-3-0)[\[2\]](#refnote-3) A [general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_elections_in_Singapore "General elections in Singapore") must be held within three months after every dissolution of Parliament. Elections are conducted by the [Elections Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_Department "Elections Department") (ELD), which is under the [Prime Minister's Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister's_Office_(Singapore) "Prime Minister's Office (Singapore)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-4-0)[\[3\]](#refnote-4)

There are 93 elected seats in Parliament organised into 14 [single-member constituencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-member_constituencies "Single-member constituencies") (SMCs) and 17 [group representation constituencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_representation_constituencies "Group representation constituencies") (GRCs). Each SMC returns one Member of Parliament using the [first-past-the-post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First-past-the-post "First-past-the-post") (FPTP) voting system, while each GRC returns four or five MPs through [party block voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_block_voting "Party block voting") (the general ticket). At least one candidate in each GRC team must be from the Malay, Indian or other minority communities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-5-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-5) A group of candidates wishing to stand for election in a GRC must all be members of the same political party, or a group of independent candidates, resulting in a situation where the victorious party obtains 100% of the seats in a given GRC. The [voting age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_age "Voting age") in Singapore is 21 years, with eligibility to voters who were born before 1 March 1999.

On 23 June 2020 at 16:00 Prime Minister [Lee Hsien Loong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Hsien_Loong "Lee Hsien Loong") announced during a live televised announcement that President [Halimah Yacob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halimah_Yacob "Halimah Yacob") had dissolved the [13th Parliament of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/13th_Parliament_of_Singapore "13th Parliament of Singapore") on the same day and had issued a writ of election with nominations to be held a week later on 30 June 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-proclamation_dissolution-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-proclamation_dissolution)[&#8202;](#ref-back-proclamation_election-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-proclamation_election)[&#8202;](#ref-back-writ-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-writ)

The [returning officer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Returning_officer "Returning officer") was Tan Meng Dui, a former deputy secretary at the [Ministry of National Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_National_Development_(Singapore) "Ministry of National Development (Singapore)") and CEO of the [National Environment Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Environment_Agency "National Environment Agency").[&#8202;](#ref-back-9-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-9)[&#8202;](#ref-back-10-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-10)[&#8202;](#ref-back-11-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-11)

### Political parties

See also: [List of political parties in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_Singapore "List of political parties in Singapore")

The governing [People's Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Action_Party "People's Action Party"), having been in power since 1959, was led by [Lee Hsien Loong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Hsien_Loong "Lee Hsien Loong") throughout the election period. The leading opposition party is the [Workers' Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers'_Party_of_Singapore "Workers' Party of Singapore") led by [Pritam Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pritam_Singh "Pritam Singh"), with six elected seats and three [NCMP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-constituency_Member_of_Parliament "Non-constituency Member of Parliament") seats before the election. A total of ten opposition parties challenged the ruling party in this election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-12-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-12)

### Changes in election process

The Elections Department introduced several new features for this election to help ease the election process for voters, candidates and election volunteers. Registration of voters on polling day will be done electronically and election officials need not manually strike out a voter's particulars from a hardcopy register of electors.[&#8202;](#ref-back-22-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-22) Voters will be able to mark their candidate(s) choices more clearly using self-inking pens and enjoy shorter queuing time with the introduction of the e-registration system. Candidates are able to fill in most of the necessary documents online while election volunteers are able to count the number of votes within a shorter duration with the help of counting machines, enabling election results to be released at least 50 minutes earlier.[&#8202;](#ref-back-23-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-23)[&#8202;](#ref-back-24-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-24) In addition, there will be more polling stations, reducing the average number of voters per polling station from 3,000 to about 2,400. Senior citizens above the age of 65 will be given priority to vote between 8am and 12pm on Polling Day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-25-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-25)

[Election recount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_recount "Election recount") can occur if the margin of results for a constituency are within a 2% range. Unlike the previous elections, the recount is now triggered automatically rather than being called out manually from the candidates or counting agents, and that only one application for a recount may be called; additionally, a standby counting machine would be used for recount instead of being manually counted. Similar to past elections, overseas votes is disregarded during a recount of votes as it does not cause any impact from the eventual results, unless if the total overseas electorate is greater than the difference of votes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-26-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-26)[&#8202;](#ref-back-27-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-27)

### Non-constituency Member of Parliament

Main article: [Non-constituency Member of Parliament (NCMP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-constituency_Member_of_Parliament "Non-constituency Member of Parliament")

On 27 January 2016, an amendment to the [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Singapore "Constitution of Singapore") was passed, increasing the minimum number of opposition Members of Parliament by three to 12 members. This is the first increase for the number of opposition MPs allocated since the [2011 Singaporean general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Singaporean_general_election "2011 Singaporean general election"), when it was increased from three members to nine.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ncmp-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-ncmp) As in the case for previous elections, NCMP seats are offered to the best-performing non-elected opposition candidates, with the number determined by the total number of opposition candidates elected; if there are at least 12 opposition candidates elected, then NCMP seats will not be offered, as previously seen in the [1991 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Singaporean_general_election "1991 Singaporean general election"). The names of the eligible candidates are announced a few days after the polling day. Candidates may decline the post if offered, as previously seen in the [1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Singaporean_general_election "1984 Singaporean general election")[&#8202;](#ref-back-29-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-29) and [2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Singaporean_general_election "2015 Singaporean general election") elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-30-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-30)[&#8202;](#ref-back-31-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-31)

### Electoral divisions

Main article: [Electoral boundary changes of the 2020 Singaporean general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_boundary_changes_of_the_2020_Singaporean_general_election "Electoral boundary changes of the 2020 Singaporean general election")

See also: [Constituencies of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituencies_of_Singapore "Constituencies of Singapore") and [List of Singaporean electoral divisions (2020-2025)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Singaporean_electoral_divisions_(2020-2025) "List of Singaporean electoral divisions (2020-2025)")

|  | 2015 | 2020 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Seats | 89 | 93 |
| Electoral divisions | 29 | 31 |
| Group representation constituencies | 16 | 17 |
| Four-Member GRCs | 6 | 6 |
| Five-Member GRCs | 8 | 11 |
| Six-Member GRCs | 2 | 0 |
| Single member constituencies | 13 | 14 |
| Average GRC size | 4.75 | 4.65 |
| Voters | 2,458,058 | 2,647,372 |
| Voters (overseas votes inclusive) | 2,462,926 | 2,653,942 |
| Average electorate per seat | 27,673 | 28,510 |

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Electoral_boundaries_during_the_Singapore_general_elections_2020.svg/1280px-Electoral_boundaries_during_the_Singapore_general_elections_2020.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Electoral_boundaries_during_the_Singapore_general_elections_2020.svg) Electoral boundaries

The Electoral Boundaries Review Committee is made up of senior civil servants and charged with adjusting the boundaries of electoral divisions prior to an election. The government officially claims this as necessary to ensure minority participation in Parliament amidst demographic changes while ensuring an equitable number of voters represented per MP, though critics have raised allegations of [gerrymandering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerrymandering "Gerrymandering") to disadvantage the opposition.[&#8202;](#ref-back-32-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-32)[&#8202;](#ref-back-33-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-33)[&#8202;](#ref-back-34-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-34) As of 2015, there were 16 GRCs and 13 SMCs. Prime Minister Lee convened the committee on 1 August 2019 with instructions to reduce the size of GRCs and increase the number of SMCs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-35-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-35)[&#8202;](#ref-back-36-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-36) The exact date of formation was revealed only when Pritam Singh asked Trade and Industry Minister [Chan Chun Sing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chan_Chun_Sing "Chan Chun Sing") in a written reply in Parliament.[&#8202;](#ref-back-37-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-37)

The Committee released its report on 13 March 2020 with the formation of 17 GRCs and 14 SMCs, increasing its seats from 89 to 93.[&#8202;](#ref-back-38-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-38)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ebrc_report-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-ebrc_report) For the first time since [1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Singaporean_general_election "1991 Singaporean general election"), there are no six-member GRCs as its only two GRCs were both reduced by one seat to five. A new [Sengkang GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sengkang_Group_Representation_Constituency "Sengkang Group Representation Constituency") was formed from portions of the former Punggol East and Sengkang West SMCs and the boundaries of Tampines GRC were altered for the first time since [2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Singaporean_general_election "2001 Singaporean general election"), due to the increase in population in the northeastern area of Singapore. Four new SMCs were also carved out ( [Kebun Baru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebun_Baru_Single_Member_Constituency "Kebun Baru Single Member Constituency"), [Yio Chu Kang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yio_Chu_Kang_Single_Member_Constituency "Yio Chu Kang Single Member Constituency"), [Marymount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marymount_Single_Member_Constituency "Marymount Single Member Constituency") and [Punggol West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punggol_West_Single_Member_Constituency "Punggol West Single Member Constituency")), three former SMCs were absorbed into neighbouring GRCs ( [Fengshan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fengshan_Single_Member_Constituency "Fengshan Single Member Constituency"), [Punggol East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punggol_East_Single_Member_Constituency "Punggol East Single Member Constituency") and [Sengkang West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sengkang_West_Single_Member_Constituency "Sengkang West Single Member Constituency")), while two SMCs ( [Hong Kah North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kah_North_SMC "Hong Kah North SMC") and [Potong Pasir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potong_Pasir_SMC "Potong Pasir SMC")) had their boundaries modified. The remaining eight SMCs and four GRCs, namely [Aljunied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aljunied_GRC "Aljunied GRC"), [Holland–Bukit Timah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holland%E2%80%93Bukit_Timah_GRC "Holland–Bukit Timah GRC"), [Jurong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurong_GRC "Jurong GRC") and [Tanjong Pagar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanjong_Pagar_GRC "Tanjong Pagar GRC"), were left untouched. The changes saw about 13% of voters being allocated to a new constituency.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ebrc_report-1)[\[38\]](#refnote-ebrc_report)

### Disruptions from COVID-19 pandemic

Main article: [COVID-19 pandemic in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Singapore "COVID-19 pandemic in Singapore")

During a _[Straits Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Straits_Times "The Straits Times")_ forum on 11 March, Deputy Prime Minister [Heng Swee Keat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heng_Swee_Keat "Heng Swee Keat") said that the timing of the elections could be affected in view of the worsening pandemic.[&#8202;](#ref-back-40-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-40) On 25 March, Senior Minister Teo Chee Hean told Parliament that he believed it would be unconstitutional for the President to form a caretaker government unless a state of emergency had been recommended by the Cabinet to the [President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Singapore "President of Singapore").[&#8202;](#ref-back-41-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-41)

On 28 March, Tan Cheng Bock responded to Teo's comments by saying that the unconstitutional nature of a caretaker government as a result of a postponing a general election would be far more preferred than having a health emergency by exposing millions of Singaporeans to potential COVID-19 infection.[&#8202;](#ref-back-42-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-42)

On 7 April, Minister for Trade and Industry Chan Chun Sing introduced the Parliamentary Elections (COVID-19 Special Arrangements) Bill to [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Singapore "Parliament of Singapore") which would allow voters under Stay-Home Notices or Quarantine Orders related to COVID-19 to vote outside of their normal electoral divisions in the upcoming General Election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-43-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-43) The Bill was passed by Parliament on 4 May and assented to by the President on 15 May.[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-44) The Parliamentary Elections (COVID-19 Special Arrangements) Act 2020 came into operation on 26 May.[&#8202;](#ref-back-45-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-45)

On 1 July, the Parliamentary Elections (COVID-19 Special Arrangements) Regulations 2020 was made to provide for mobile polling teams at special polling stations and to allow voters who have fever or on COVID-19 stay orders to vote from 7pm to 8pm on polling day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-PECOVID-19SAR2020-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-PECOVID-19SAR2020)

#### Restrictions on election campaigning

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/GE2020_PAP_and_SPP_campaign_posters_in_Bishan-Toa_Payoh_GRC.jpg/500px-GE2020_PAP_and_SPP_campaign_posters_in_Bishan-Toa_Payoh_GRC.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:GE2020_PAP_and_SPP_campaign_posters_in_Bishan-Toa_Payoh_GRC.jpg) PAP and SPP campaign posters during the GE2020.

On 18 June, the ELD introduced temporary measures to reduce the risk of transmission of COVID-19, such as no rallies and TV screenings pertaining to the election are to be held, instead replacing with e-rallies and a new "Constituency Political Broadcast",[&#8202;](#ref-back-47-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-47) and nomination centres will no longer admit members of the public or supporters during nomination day. Walkabouts and campaigning vehicles are still allowed, though safe distancing and minimal physical contact still applies, and candidates are also not allowed to make speeches or physical rallies, including during the campaigning from campaigning vehicles, meaning that there will be no parades held by the candidates after the election,[&#8202;](#ref-back-48-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-48) though it can still broadcast any pre-recorded messages.[&#8202;](#ref-back-campaignvehicleST-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-campaignvehicleST)[&#8202;](#ref-back-campaignvehicleTODAY-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-campaignvehicleTODAY)

#### Restrictions during polling day

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/GE2020_Poll_Card_Toa_Payoh-Bishan_GRC.jpg/500px-GE2020_Poll_Card_Toa_Payoh-Bishan_GRC.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:GE2020_Poll_Card_Toa_Payoh-Bishan_GRC.jpg) A polling card which included a recommended two-hour time slot.

Voters were given a recommended two-hour time slot to cast their vote on Polling Day (from 8am to 8pm) on their ballot card as a measure to counter long queues during polling. Measures for safe distancing still apply on polling day. Voters with fever or respiratory symptoms would be denied entry from the polling stations, except during the last hour (7pm to 8pm).[&#8202;](#ref-back-howtovote-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-howtovote)[&#8202;](#ref-back-PECOVID-19SAR2020-1)[\[45\]](#refnote-PECOVID-19SAR2020) Voters who are unable to vote because of medical issues, or because they are overseas, will be delisted from the registrar of voters due to [mandatory voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compulsory_voting "Compulsory voting"), but will not need to pay the $50 fee for reinstating their names. Voters had to use hand sanitizer before voting, and were initially required to wear disposible gloves, but this was later changed due to the requirement causing long queues.[&#8202;](#ref-back-52-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-52)[&#8202;](#ref-back-53-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-53) Elderly voters above the age of 65 were given a special time slot in the morning. The restrictions caused long queues at polling stations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-54-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-54)

For overseas voting (which was held in ten cities, [Dubai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dubai "Dubai"), [London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London "London"), [Tokyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokyo "Tokyo"), [Beijing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing "Beijing"), [Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington,_D.C. "Washington, D.C."), [Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong "Hong Kong"), [Shanghai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai "Shanghai"), [San Francisco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco "San Francisco"), [New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City "New York City") and [Canberra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canberra "Canberra")), voting was subject to the approval of the authorities in the affected countries, while ELD announced that they would release the arrangements for returning Singaporeans who are issued a mandatory 14-day [stay home notice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stay-at-home_order "Stay-at-home order") to vote in hotels and reducing the risk of infecting others.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ELD_overseas_voting-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-ELD_overseas_voting)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ELD_special_voting-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-ELD_special_voting) These arrangements were later officially announced on 1 July, when the Singapore government announced that special polling stations will be set up in [Marina Bay Sands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marina_Bay_Sands "Marina Bay Sands") and [JW Marriott Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JW_Marriott_Hotels "JW Marriott Hotels") Singapore South Beach to accommodate voters serving their stay-home notices in the two hotels.[&#8202;](#ref-back-57-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-57)[&#8202;](#ref-back-58-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-58)

350 voters who are quarantined at the time did not vote as they are not allowed to leave their place where they were currently isolated to cast their vote.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ELD_quarantine-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-ELD_quarantine)

### Extension of polling hours

The polls were slated to close at 8pm; however, in an unprecedented move in Singapore electoral history, the Elections Department extended the voting hours till 10pm, just within two hours before its initial closing time. According to the Elections Department, there were long queues at some polling stations, and the extension was to "allow enough time for all voters to cast their votes".[&#8202;](#ref-back-60-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-60)[&#8202;](#ref-back-61-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-61) This drew criticism from several opposition parties for compromising the integrity of the election, as some were unable to field a polling agent to supervise the sealing of ballot boxes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-62-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-62) The Elections Department replied that polling and counting procedures will continue as normal.[&#8202;](#ref-back-63-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-63)

## Political developments

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/01/Heng_Swee_Keat.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Heng_Swee_Keat.jpg) Heng Swee Keat was widely tipped to succeed Lee Hsien Loong as the next prime minister of Singapore; he withdrew himself from the nomination in 2021; Heng would retire from politics in 2025.

### People's Action Party

On 13 March 2016, the PAP MP for [Bukit Batok SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukit_Batok_Single_Member_Constituency "Bukit Batok Single Member Constituency") [David Ong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Ong "David Ong") resigned over personal indiscretions with a party grassroots activist. In the subsequent [Bukit Batok by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Bukit_Batok_by-election "2016 Bukit Batok by-election"), the PAP candidate and former Aljunied GRC candidate [Murali Pillai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murali_Pillai "Murali Pillai") defeated the opposition SDP candidate Chee Soon Juan to reclaim the seat for the PAP.[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-64)

In the lead up to the [2017 Singapore presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Singaporean_presidential_election "2017 Singaporean presidential election"), [Speaker of Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_Parliament_of_Singapore "Speaker of the Parliament of Singapore") and MP for [Marsiling–Yew Tee GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsiling%E2%80%93Yew_Tee_Group_Representation_Constituency "Marsiling–Yew Tee Group Representation Constituency") Halimah Yacob resigned from the party on 7 August 2017 to fulfil regulations which prohibit the [President of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Singapore "President of Singapore") from any party affiliations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-65) As she had vacated her seat in Parliament, there were calls for a by-election, though the Court of Appeal eventually ruled that there was "no duty to call a by-election when a single vacancy arises in a GRC".[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-66)

On 23 November 2018, Heng Swee Keat and Chan Chun Sing were respectively elected as the party's first and second assistant secretary-general to the PAP's 35th Central Executive Committee (CEC).[&#8202;](#ref-back-67-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-67) The two assistant posts were seen as an indicator of Lee Hsien Loong's successor, given that the upcoming election is likely to be Lee's last.[&#8202;](#ref-back-68-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-68) Following the announcement of Heng Swee Keat's appointment as the sole [deputy prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_Singapore "Deputy Prime Minister of Singapore") on 1 May 2019, succeeding [Teo Chee Hean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teo_Chee_Hean "Teo Chee Hean") and [Tharman Shanmugaratnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tharman_Shanmugaratnam "Tharman Shanmugaratnam"), former MP [Inderjit Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inderjit_Singh_(Singaporean_politician) "Inderjit Singh (Singaporean politician)") said that the PAP intends to showcase Heng's imminent ascent to the top post and dispel rumours of any surprises in the leadership succession.[&#8202;](#ref-back-69-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-69)[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-70)

### Workers' Party

On 3 November 2017, then-secretary-general and Aljunied GRC incumbent MP [Low Thia Khiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_Thia_Khiang "Low Thia Khiang") announced during a speech on the party's 60th anniversary that he would not contest the next party elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-71) During the party's 2016 CEC election, [Chen Show Mao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Show_Mao "Chen Show Mao") had mounted a surprise challenge for the leadership post but lost the election to Low in a 41–65 vote. In the leadership election on 8 April 2018, Pritam Singh was elected unopposed as successor to Low as the party's new secretary-general.[&#8202;](#ref-back-72-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-72)

Observers say that the lack of a contest was a good sign of internal unity, and that the party is keen to demonstrate its multiracial credentials now that it has a minority secretary-general, for the first time since 2001 with [J. B. Jeyaretnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._B._Jeyaretnam "J. B. Jeyaretnam").[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-73)

On 30 April 2020, Low was hospitalised due to a head injury, and was discharged on 21 May.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LTKICU-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-LTKICU)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LTKDSG-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-LTKDSG) On 25 June, while Low was on rehabilitation, the party announced he (and along with Chen and [Png Eng Huat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Png_Eng_Huat "Png Eng Huat")) will not seek election for the first time in his 32-year career, though he has not ruled out his retirement from politics;[&#8202;](#ref-back-76-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-76) in his interview, he cited his satisfaction on his leadership, saying that 'I felt my work was done'.[&#8202;](#ref-back-77-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-77)[&#8202;](#ref-back-78-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-78)[&#8202;](#ref-back-79-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-79) It would be the last election where Low was active as he announced his political retirement on 7 December 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-80-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-80)[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-81)

### New parties

- [2011 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Singaporean_presidential_election "2011 Singaporean presidential election") candidate and former [Ayer Rajah SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayer_Rajah_Single_Member_Constituency "Ayer Rajah Single Member Constituency") MP [Tan Cheng Bock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tan_Cheng_Bock "Tan Cheng Bock") returned to politics under the [Progress Singapore Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progress_Singapore_Party "Progress Singapore Party") that he founded together with several previously contested candidates on 18 January 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-82) The PSP was registered by the Registrar of Societies on 28 March 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-83-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-83) The PSP announced on 26 June 2020 its line-up of candidates for the nine constituencies it will be contesting, with party chief Tan Cheng Bock leading a team in West Coast GRC, a ward that has his former constituency of Ayer Rajah.[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-84)
- Former leader of the [National Solidarity Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Solidarity_Party_(Singapore) "National Solidarity Party (Singapore)"), [Lim Tean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lim_Tean "Lim Tean") founded [Peoples Voice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoples_Voice_(Singapore) "Peoples Voice (Singapore)") which was registered by the Registrar of Societies on 31 October 2018.[&#8202;](#ref-back-85-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-85)[&#8202;](#ref-back-86-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-86)
- On 29 May 2020, [Ravi Philemon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ravi_Philemon "Ravi Philemon") and Michelle Lee Juen submitted an application to register a new political party, [Red Dot United](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Dot_United "Red Dot United") (RDU), with the Registrar of Societies. Prior to the foundation, they were former members from the Progress Singapore Party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-87) The party was registered on 15 June 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-88-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-88)

### Extraparliamentary parties

- [Singapore Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Democratic_Party "Singapore Democratic Party") began campaigning on 23 February 2019,[&#8202;](#ref-back-89-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-89) and announced a few key campaign agendas over March and May that year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-90-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-90)[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-91) Ahead of the election, the party recruited ex- [DPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Progressive_Party_(Singapore) "Democratic Progressive Party (Singapore)") leader [Benjamin Pwee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Pwee "Benjamin Pwee").[&#8202;](#ref-back-92-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-92)
- On 16 October 2019, the [Singapore People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_People's_Party "Singapore People's Party") saw its first major leadership change since its formation with opposition veteran [Chiam See Tong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiam_See_Tong "Chiam See Tong") stepping down as secretary-general of the SPP after holding the post for 23 years since December 1996,[&#8202;](#ref-back-93-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-93) with former [Non-constituency Member of Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-constituency_Member_of_Parliament "Non-constituency Member of Parliament") [Steve Chia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Chia "Steve Chia") succeeding Chiam on 5 November 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-94)
- On 3 January 2020, four political parties ( [Singaporeans First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singaporeans_First "Singaporeans First"), [People's Power Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Power_Party_(Singapore) "People's Power Party (Singapore)"), [Reform Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Party_(Singapore) "Reform Party (Singapore)") and [Democratic Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Progressive_Party_(Singapore) "Democratic Progressive Party (Singapore)")) formally agreed to contest in the election as an alliance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-95) Three months later, on 1 April, the four parties decided to join the [Singapore Democratic Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Democratic_Alliance "Singapore Democratic Alliance") instead;[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-96) however, the SDA has since placed their membership applications on hold, though it seeks to maintain an informal alliance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-97)[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-98)[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-99) On 25 June, [Singaporeans First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singaporeans_First "Singaporeans First")'s chief [Tan Jee Say](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tan_Jee_Say "Tan Jee Say") announced the party's dissolution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-singfirst_dissolved-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-singfirst_dissolved) On 27 June, the Democratic Progressive Party announced its withdrawal from contesting the election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-DPPQuit-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-DPPQuit)

## Political issues

### Battleground constituencies

Many media outlets such as [8world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8world "8world"), [CNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNA_(TV_network) "CNA (TV network)"), _[The Straits Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Straits_Times "The Straits Times")_,[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-102) _[Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Today_(website) "Today (website)")_ and [Mothership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mothership_(website) "Mothership (website)")[&#8202;](#ref-back-103-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-103) named several constituencies as its "hotspot" states for the election:

- [Aljunied GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aljunied_GRC "Aljunied GRC") (2% margin of WP's 2015 victory, Low's departure from the party leadership, and Aljunied–Hougang Town Council's funding scandals)[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-104)
- [Bukit Batok SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukit_Batok_SMC "Bukit Batok SMC") and [Bukit Panjang SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukit_Panjang_SMC "Bukit Panjang SMC") (respectively contested by SDP leaders [Chee Soon Juan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chee_Soon_Juan "Chee Soon Juan") and [Paul Tambyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Tambyah "Paul Tambyah"))[&#8202;](#ref-back-105-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-105)[&#8202;](#ref-back-106-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-106)
- [East Coast GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Coast_GRC "East Coast GRC") (WP's strong performance in 2011 and 2015, which granted the party NCMP seats, including in the defunct [Fengshan SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fengshan_SMC "Fengshan SMC"))[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-107)
- [Marine Parade GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Parade_GRC "Marine Parade GRC") (WP contest and the retirement of [Goh Chok Tong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goh_Chok_Tong "Goh Chok Tong"))[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-108)
- [Sengkang GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sengkang_GRC "Sengkang GRC") (new constituency covering prior WP-contested constituencies in the [Sengkang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sengkang "Sengkang") planning area, including [Punggol East SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punggol_East_SMC "Punggol East SMC"))[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-109)
- [West Coast GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_GRC "West Coast GRC") (PSP's contest, notably Tan Cheng Bock's strong performance in [Ayer Rajah SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayer_Rajah_SMC "Ayer Rajah SMC"), now part of the constituency)[&#8202;](#ref-back-110-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-110)

### Criticisms of the government's response to the COVID-19 pandemic

Many opposition parties criticized the PAP government's handling of the pandemic. SDP's chairman [Paul Tambyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Tambyah "Paul Tambyah"), who is an infectious disease expert and president-elect of the [International Society for Infectious Diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Society_for_Infectious_Diseases "International Society for Infectious Diseases"), claimed that the government was more concerned over a shortfall of masks than public health when it issued advisories not to wear masks unless a person was unwell. Peoples Voice chief Lim Tean also charged that the PAP had failed to act with "speed and decisiveness" despite having reportedly learnt the lessons from [SARS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS "SARS") 17 years ago. PSP candidate Ang Yong Guan questioned the belated "one-month delay" response after a foreign worker was admitted to hospital, which saw the virus rapidly spreading within their dormitories unchecked, leading to Singapore having the highest number of cases within [ASEAN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ASEAN "ASEAN").[&#8202;](#ref-back-111-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-111) PSP chief Tan Cheng Bock, in his 2 July Party Political Broadcast said the PAP government's "boasting in January failed to prepare Singapore for the explosion of dormitory cases in April."[&#8202;](#ref-back-112-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-112)

### 10 million population controversy

During the English-language debate, SDP Secretary-General [Chee Soon Juan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chee_Soon_Juan "Chee Soon Juan") touched on one of its campaign promises of saying no to a 10million population in Singapore, citing a 2019 article from _[The Straits Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Straits_Times "The Straits Times")_, to which PAP representative [Vivian Balakrishnan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vivian_Balakrishnan "Vivian Balakrishnan") replied that the [Prime Minister's Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister's_Office_(Singapore) "Prime Minister's Office (Singapore)") had issued a statement that day "advising people like you not to indulge in falsehoods" and denied that there would be a population of 10million in Singapore, adding that the figure was a "strawman".[&#8202;](#ref-back-1JulyFullDebateCNA-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-1JulyFullDebateCNA)

PAP candidate and Deputy Prime Minister Heng Swee Keat denied saying that Singapore should plan to increase its population to 10million people. He reiterated the Singapore government's stance that it had "never proposed or targeted for Singapore to increase the population to 10 million". PAP candidate Vivian Balakrishnan also called for the SDP to clarify its claim. Nevertheless, the SDP later claimed victory for pressuring the PAP into declaring that it did not have a population target of 10million, to which a PAP spokesman denounced as a "falsehood" which "renders the campaign pointless, and calls into question the integrity of the whole party".[&#8202;](#ref-back-114-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-114) The National Population and Talent Division of the Prime Minister's Office also objected to the claim.[&#8202;](#ref-back-115-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-115)

The dispute between the PAP and the SDP continued, with Paul Tambyah and Chee Soon Juan defending SDP's decision to press the PAP about plans for a population target of 10million for Singapore, saying that PAP was now forced to clarify and assure Singaporeans that there are no such plans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-116-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-116) Paul pointed out that PAP could have clarified the matter earlier but did not do so, adding that he was "baffled" that the PAP called the target a "falsehood" perpetuated by the SDP.[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-117) Both WP and PSP also reacted to the dispute; WP Secretary-General Pritam Singh said that there was "room for fair comment" as it was not clear if SDP's stance could be considered a "falsehood" or not; however he did not endorse either side and said that it was a matter that WP was not involved in. Separately, PSP Secretary-General Tan Cheng Bock said that the issue came about due to a lack of transparency from the Singapore government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-118-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-118) Heng Swee Keat claimed that the SDP had "erected a [bogeyman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bogeyman "Bogeyman")" and said that he was expecting integrity and honesty from all candidates contesting in the election, to which Tambyah said that the PAP "should take up any unhappiness it may have over the claim with _[The Straits Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Straits_Times "The Straits Times")_", adding that Singaporeans should read the article concerned to make their own judgements.[&#8202;](#ref-back-119-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-119) PAP later issued a statement, saying that it was "disappointed but not surprised" at SDP's response, and claimed that "the SDP have dug their heels in, repeated their falsehoods and refused to apologise to Singaporeans for misleading them" and that "Dr Chee has not changed, cannot change and will never change".[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-120)[&#8202;](#ref-back-121-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-121)

Paul Tambyah and Chee Soon Juan responded to PAP's statement issued the previous day; Tambyah said that PAP's comments were "a sign of desperation" and that it showed that PAP had "run out of ideas" and "resorted to the old PAP tactics of just politics of personal destruction", while Chee said PAP was "beating a dead horse".[&#8202;](#ref-back-122-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-122) Nevertheless, the two SDP leaders said that it was time for the election campaign to move on from the dispute.[&#8202;](#ref-back-123-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-123)[&#8202;](#ref-back-124-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-124) In addition, the [Association of Women for Action and Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Women_for_Action_and_Research "Association of Women for Action and Research") criticised PAP's statement to SDP the previous day as PAP had used an analogy involving [spousal abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_violence "Domestic violence") to make a point.[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-125) Both SDP and PV's Facebook pages, as well as several other Facebook pages belonging to others, were issued correction directions by the alternate authority of the [Protection from Online Falsehoods and Manipulation Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protection_from_Online_Falsehoods_and_Manipulation_Act "Protection from Online Falsehoods and Manipulation Act") Office over the claim of plans for a population target of 10million for Singapore.[&#8202;](#ref-back-126-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-126)[&#8202;](#ref-back-127-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-127)

### Opposition wipeout concerns

In introducing WP's manifesto, Pritam Singh stated that there was a "real risk" of a wipeout of elected opposition MPs, adding that WP candidates must be voted into Parliament to serve the public in Parliament and strengthen democracy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-WP_launches_manifesto-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-WP_launches_manifesto) PAP Secretary-General Lee Hsien Loong said that the possibility was an unrealistic outcome, claiming that Singh's argument was "a tactic" and added that he was "using [reverse psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reverse_psychology "Reverse psychology")".[&#8202;](#ref-back-129-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-129) Two other PAP incumbent candidates, Chan Chun Sing and [Indranee Rajah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indranee_Rajah "Indranee Rajah"), also responded to Singh's point, saying that it would be a mistake for voters to think that the PAP would return to power effortlessly, while adding that it was important for the PAP to get a "clear and strong endorsement" from the people.[&#8202;](#ref-back-130-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-130)

### Allegations of discriminatory comments by Raeesah Khan

On 5 July, reports emerged that two separate police reports were lodged against WP's Sengkang GRC candidate [Raeesah Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raeesah_Khan "Raeesah Khan") for her online comments, which allegedly promoted enmity between different religious and racial groups. Her Facebook post in May 2020 had criticised the Singapore law enforcement authorities for discriminating against Singaporean citizens and said that "rich Chinese" and "white people" were treated differently under the law, which was largely based on the differences in sentences handed out to individuals who broke the ["circuit breaker" measures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Singapore_circuit_breaker_measures "2020 Singapore circuit breaker measures") in the midst of the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic "COVID-19 pandemic"), and another Facebook post in February 2018 focused on the [2018 City Harvest Church ruling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Harvest_Church_Criminal_Breach_of_Trust_Case "City Harvest Church Criminal Breach of Trust Case").[&#8202;](#ref-back-131-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-131)[&#8202;](#ref-back-132-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-132)[&#8202;](#ref-back-133-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-133)[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-134) WP swiftly came out to support Raeesah; in a press conference with WP chief Pritam Singh, chairperson Sylvia Lim and the Sengkang GRC team, Raeesah also apologised and released a statement stating that her intention was "never to cause social divisions but to raise awareness on minority issues", adding that she also regretted making her "insensitive" comments.[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-135)[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-136)[&#8202;](#ref-back-137-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-137)

On 7 July, PAP released a statement accusing Raeesah of admitting to "making highly derogatory statements about Chinese and Christians" and asked "Why does the WP still consider her worthy of consideration as an MP?"[&#8202;](#ref-back-138-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-138) PSP chief Tan Cheng Bock weighed in on the controversy and accused the PAP of engaging in "gutter politics". He questioned the PAP to "look at themselves and see whether they have conducted themselves in a gentlemanly way".[&#8202;](#ref-back-139-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-139) Similarly, the police revealed that they were investigating the man who allegedly reported Raeesah over social media comments which intended to wound religious and racial feelings.[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-140)

On 17 September, the [Singapore Police Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Police_Force "Singapore Police Force") announced that it had concluded its investigations into the two incidents, and that aside from a "stern warning" to both Raeesah and the blogger; neither action was taken against both.[&#8202;](#ref-back-141-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-141)[&#8202;](#ref-back-142-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-142)[&#8202;](#ref-back-143-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-143)[&#8202;](#ref-back-144-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-144)

### Allegations of racist comments by Heng Swee Keat

A series of police reports were made in reaction to the first police report, including one against [Heng Swee Keat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heng_Swee_Keat "Heng Swee Keat"), who had remarked that Singaporeans were "not ready for a prime minister from a minority race" or someone that was non-Chinese, and one against the People's Action Party's press statement on WP Raeesah Khan, both on the grounds of promoting enmity between different religious or racial groups.[&#8202;](#ref-back-145-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-145)[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-146) In a statement on 8 July, the police deemed that no offence had been committed by the PAP. The [Singapore Police Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Police_Force "Singapore Police Force"), after consultation with the [Attorney-General's Chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney-General_of_Singapore "Attorney-General of Singapore"), stated that "Mr Heng's remarks, in the context they were made, do not evidence any intent to wound anyone's racial feelings or promote enmity between different races". Since news broke of the statement released by the police, netizens have spoken out about how they felt Heng's statement was racist.[&#8202;](#ref-back-147-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-147)

### New and outgoing candidates

Main article: [Pre-election day events of the 2020 Singaporean general election §New candidates/Outgoing MPs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-election_day_events_of_the_2020_Singaporean_general_election#New_candidates/Outgoing_MPs "Pre-election day events of the 2020 Singaporean general election")

There were a total 73 new candidates participated in the election, among which include the nation's first female [brigadier-general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brigadier-general "Brigadier-general"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-162-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-162) a few former civil servants,[&#8202;](#ref-back-163-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-163)[&#8202;](#ref-back-164-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-164) and a [MRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_Rapid_Transit_(Singapore) "Mass Rapid Transit (Singapore)") train announcer[&#8202;](#ref-back-MRT-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-MRT) who is also a candidate previously withdrew from participating in the previous election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-166-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-166)[&#8202;](#ref-back-167-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-167)

23 candidates (three were from the Workers' Party) did not stand for candidacy, among which were Emeritus [Senior Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senior_Minister_of_Singapore "Senior Minister of Singapore") and second prime minister [Goh Chok Tong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goh_Chok_Tong "Goh Chok Tong") who stepped down from politics after 44 years,[&#8202;](#ref-back-168-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-168) Former [leader of the opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Singapore) "Leader of the Opposition (Singapore)") Low Thia Khiang (the longest serving opposition MP at 29 years),[&#8202;](#ref-back-WP3-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-WP3) Transport Minister [Khaw Boon Wan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaw_Boon_Wan "Khaw Boon Wan"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-170-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-170) and former ministers [Lim Hng Kiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lim_Hng_Kiang "Lim Hng Kiang"), [Lim Swee Say](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lim_Swee_Say "Lim Swee Say") and [Yaacob Ibrahim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaacob_Ibrahim "Yaacob Ibrahim").[&#8202;](#ref-back-171-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-171)

### Pre-nomination day events

Main article: [Pre-election day events of the 2020 Singaporean general election §Pre-nomination day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-election_day_events_of_the_2020_Singaporean_general_election#Pre-nomination_day "Pre-election day events of the 2020 Singaporean general election")

Between the start of election proceedings in March 2020 and nomination day on 30 June 2020, various political parties in Singapore made preparations to contest in the general election by revealing their candidates and manifesto to the general public.[&#8202;](#ref-back-172-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-172)[&#8202;](#ref-back-173-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-173)[&#8202;](#ref-back-WP_launches_manifesto-1)[\[127\]](#refnote-WP_launches_manifesto)[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-174)[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-175)

### Nomination day

On 30 June 2020 from 11am to 12 noon SGT, each candidate filed their nomination papers (along with the approval of a proposer, a seconder and at least four assentors), a political donation certificate (by before 26 June), and paid an [election deposit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_deposit "Election deposit") of [S$](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_dollar "Singapore dollar") 13,500 (down from S$14,500 in the previous 2015 election, but also the same amount as seen in the [2016 by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukit_Batok_by-election,_2016 "Bukit Batok by-election, 2016")) in one of the nine designated schools or through online to complete their application. Additionally, in the case for Group Representation Constituencies, their team must consist of at least one minority candidate and must also submit a community committee form (Malay/Muslim or Indian/other minority which is dependent on the constituency's requirements).[&#8202;](#ref-back-ELD_Press_Release-2)[\[151\]](#refnote-ELD_Press_Release)

The list of nine schools designated as nomination centres were:[&#8202;](#ref-back-writ-3)[\[7\]](#refnote-writ)

| School | Participating constituencies |
| --- | --- |
| Bendemeer Primary School | [Bishan–Toa Payoh GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishan%E2%80%93Toa_Payoh_GRC "Bishan–Toa Payoh GRC") M, [Jalan Besar GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalan_Besar_GRC "Jalan Besar GRC") M, [Radin Mas SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radin_Mas_SMC "Radin Mas SMC"), [Tanjong Pagar GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanjong_Pagar_GRC "Tanjong Pagar GRC") IO |
| Chongfu School | [Kebun Baru SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebun_Baru_Single_Member_Constituency "Kebun Baru Single Member Constituency"), [Marsiling–Yew Tee GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsiling%E2%80%93Yew_Tee_GRC "Marsiling–Yew Tee GRC") M, [Nee Soon GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nee_Soon_GRC "Nee Soon GRC") IO, [Sembawang GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sembawang_GRC "Sembawang GRC") M |
| [Deyi Secondary School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deyi_Secondary_School "Deyi Secondary School") | [Aljunied GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aljunied_GRC "Aljunied GRC") M, [Ang Mo Kio GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ang_Mo_Kio_GRC "Ang Mo Kio GRC") IO, [Marymount SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marymount_Single_Member_Constituency "Marymount Single Member Constituency"), [Yio Chu Kang SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yio_Chu_Kang_SMC "Yio Chu Kang SMC") |
| [Jurong Pioneer Junior College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurong_Pioneer_Junior_College "Jurong Pioneer Junior College") | [Chua Chu Kang GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chua_Chu_Kang_GRC "Chua Chu Kang GRC") M, [Hong Kah North SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kah_North_SMC "Hong Kah North SMC"), [Pioneer SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pioneer_SMC "Pioneer SMC") |
| [Kong Hwa School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kong_Hwa_School "Kong Hwa School") | [MacPherson SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacPherson_SMC "MacPherson SMC"), [Marine Parade GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Parade_GRC "Marine Parade GRC") M, [Mountbatten SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountbatten_SMC "Mountbatten SMC"), [Potong Pasir SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potong_Pasir_SMC "Potong Pasir SMC") |
| [Methodist Girls’ School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist_Girls'_School,_Singapore "Methodist Girls' School, Singapore") | [Bukit Panjang SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukit_Panjang_SMC "Bukit Panjang SMC"), [Holland–Bukit Timah GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holland%E2%80%93Bukit_Timah_GRC "Holland–Bukit Timah GRC") IO |
| [Nan Hua High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nan_Hua_High_School "Nan Hua High School") | [Bukit Batok SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukit_Batok_SMC "Bukit Batok SMC"), [Jurong GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurong_GRC "Jurong GRC") IO, [West Coast GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_GRC "West Coast GRC") IO, [Yuhua SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuhua_SMC "Yuhua SMC") |
| Poi Ching School | [Hougang SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hougang_SMC "Hougang SMC"), [Tampines GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampines_GRC "Tampines GRC") M |
| St Anthony's Canossian Primary School | [East Coast GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Coast_GRC "East Coast GRC") M, [Pasir Ris–Punggol GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasir_Ris%E2%80%93Punggol_GRC "Pasir Ris–Punggol GRC") M, [Punggol West SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punggol_West_Single_Member_Constituency "Punggol West Single Member Constituency"), [Sengkang GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sengkang_GRC "Sengkang GRC") M |

- M indicates a GRC requires a Malay/Muslim minority candidate
- IO indicates a GRC requires an Indian or other minority candidate

As in the case of previous elections, candidates will lose their deposit if they are unable to garner at least one-eighth (12.5%) of the valid votes cast within the contested constituency.

Following nomination day, all 93 seats were contested by 192 candidates from 11 parties, the most ever in the history of independent Singapore, surpassing the record set from the [2015 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Singaporean_general_election "2015 Singaporean general election"), and thus become the second consecutive election not to have a [walkover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walkover "Walkover") in any constituency.[&#8202;](#ref-back-GE2020noms-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-GE2020noms) There were also a record number of female candidates participating in the election, with 40 of them contesting.[&#8202;](#ref-back-177-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-177) Only two constituencies, [Pasir Ris–Punggol GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasir_Ris%E2%80%93Punggol_GRC "Pasir Ris–Punggol GRC") and [Pioneer SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pioneer_SMC "Pioneer SMC"), saw a three-cornered fight, with the former being the first multi-cornered contest inside a Group Representation Constituency since the [1992 Marine Parade by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Marine_Parade_by-election "1992 Marine Parade by-election") 28 years prior.[&#8202;](#ref-back-PRP-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-PRP)

### Pre-polling day events

Main article: [Pre-election day events of the 2020 Singaporean general election §Pre-polling day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-election_day_events_of_the_2020_Singaporean_general_election#Pre-polling_day "Pre-election day events of the 2020 Singaporean general election")

Between nomination day (30 June 2020) and polling day (10 July 2020), candidates began campaigning on various media platforms. Online e-rallies were held in light of the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Singapore "COVID-19 pandemic in Singapore"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-LiveStreamST-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-LiveStreamST) and political broadcasts (by party and by constituency) were aired on national television.[&#8202;](#ref-back-180-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-180) Two round table political debates, one in English and the other in Mandarin, were also held.[&#8202;](#ref-back-1JulyFullDebateCNA2-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-1JulyFullDebateCNA2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-1JulyDebateCNA-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-1JulyDebateCNA) On the eve of polling day also known as [cooling-off day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_silence "Election silence"), campaigning was prohibited except for party political broadcasts.[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-183)

## Results

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Full_map_of_the_results_of_the_Singaporean_general_election_2020.svg/1280px-Full_map_of_the_results_of_the_Singaporean_general_election_2020.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Full_map_of_the_results_of_the_Singaporean_general_election_2020.svg) Map of the results of the 2020 Singapore general election

Polls closed at 10pm and vote counting began soon after. The results were announced by the [returning officer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Returning_officer "Returning officer") Tan Meng Dui, who is also CEO of the [National Environment Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Environment_Agency "National Environment Agency").

Similar to the [2015 Singaporean general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Singaporean_general_election "2015 Singaporean general election") and [2016 by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukit_Batok_by-election,_2016 "Bukit Batok by-election, 2016"), [sample counts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sample_(statistics) "Sample (statistics)") were released by the Elections Department prior to the announcement of the actual results to prevent any unnecessary speculation or reliance on unofficial sources of information while counting was still under way.[&#8202;](#ref-back-184-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-184)[&#8202;](#ref-back-185-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-185)

The first results were announced at 1:22am for [Bukit Panjang SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukit_Panjang_SMC "Bukit Panjang SMC") where the [PAP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Action_Party "People's Action Party") was elected with 53.74% of the vote. The last results came at 3:44am, when [Aljunied GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aljunied_GRC "Aljunied GRC") and [Nee Soon GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nee_Soon_GRC "Nee Soon GRC") were announced to have been retained by WP and the PAP with 59.95% and 61.90% respectively.

The WP  broke the record for overall contested vote share for any opposition party with 50.49% of the votes, surpassing the previous record of 48.55% held by the [Singapore Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Democratic_Party "Singapore Democratic Party") in [1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Singaporean_general_election "1991 Singaporean general election"). Six candidates, one from a SMC and five from a GRC, each lost their $13,500 deposit in the election, the biggest number of candidates to do so since the [1980 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Singaporean_general_election "1980 Singaporean general election"). Furthermore, out of 40 female candidates, 28 women (including four opposition members) were elected as MPs, setting a record for the most ever female MPs in parliament, at 29% of the chamber, including its first female minority opposition and youngest MP-elect at 26 years of age, [Raeesah Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raeesah_Khan "Raeesah Khan").[&#8202;](#ref-back-186-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-186)

In terms of swings, all but two existing constituencies ( [MacPherson SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacPherson_SMC "MacPherson SMC") and [Mountbatten SMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountbatten_SMC "Mountbatten SMC")) had anti-PAP swings. Big swings were seen in most constituencies of western Singapore from challenges by either the PSP or SDP, with the most notable one being [West Coast GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_GRC "West Coast GRC"), where it had the largest swing in this election of 26.88%, which helped the PSP win two [non-constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-constituency_Member_of_Parliament "Non-constituency Member of Parliament") seats even as the PAP retained the constituency with only 51.69% of the vote. They were taken up by Hazel Poa and [Leong Mun Wai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leong_Mun_Wai "Leong Mun Wai") for achieving the best-performing non-elected result of any opposition slate, which was confirmed on 14 July.[&#8202;](#ref-back-PSPNCMP-1)[\[155\]](#refnote-PSPNCMP) For the second consecutive election, the PAP [safe seat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safe_seat "Safe seat") of [Jurong GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurong_GRC "Jurong GRC") saw the best result of the PAP, or any party, in any constituency in the election, with 74.62% of the vote.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Jurong-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-Jurong)

Excluding the overseas electorate, voter turnout was 95.54%, or 2,535,565 voters, the highest turnout rate of any election (including both general and presidential elections) since the [1997 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Singaporean_general_election "1997 Singaporean general election"), when 95.91% voted.[&#8202;](#ref-back-188-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-188) 1.81% of votes cast were invalid votes, the lowest rate in a general election in independent Singapore, and the lowest rate since the [1963 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_Singaporean_general_election "1963 Singaporean general election") when 0.99% were invalid (when Singapore was still a state in Malaysia).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Rejected-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-Rejected)

Popular vote

1. PAP (61.2%)
2. WP (11.2%)
3. PSP (10.2%)
4. SDP (4.45%)
5. NSP (3.75%)
6. Others (9.15%)

Elected seats won

1. 83 seats (PAP) (89.2%)
2. 10 seats (WP) (10.8%)

### By Community Development Councils

With regards to [Community Development Councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_Development_Council "Community Development Council") (CDCs), the PAP maintained a strong foothold in the [Central CDC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Singapore_Community_Development_Council "Central Singapore Community Development Council"), capturing 66.6% of the valid votes. This success was largely driven by heavy-hitting constituencies like [Ang Mo Kio GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ang_Mo_Kio_Group_Representation_Constituency "Ang Mo Kio Group Representation Constituency"), the stronghold of [Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Singapore "Prime Minister of Singapore") [Lee Hsien Loong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Hsien_Loong "Lee Hsien Loong"). In contrast, the WP mounted a fierce challenge in [North East CDC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_East_Community_Development_Council "North East Community Development Council"), with the PAP's vote share there dipping to 54.8%, while the WP claimed 28.1%. Together, the Central and North East CDCs represented the largest electorate block during the election, accounting for 44 out of the 93 available parliamentary seats (47%).

The PAP's weaker showing in the North East dragged down its national average, causing its nationwide vote share to fall to 61.2%. However, strong performances in the west kept the ruling party's national numbers from dropping further. The PAP secured 64.0% of the vote in the [North West CDC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_West_Community_Development_Council "North West Community Development Council") and 61.4% in the [South West CDC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_West_Community_Development_Council "South West Community Development Council"), compared to a lower 58.5% in the [South East CDC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_East_Community_Development_Council "South East Community Development Council"). While the PSP mounted a fierce challenge and nearly captured [West Coast GRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_Group_Representation_Constituency "West Coast Group Representation Constituency"), the bulk of its electoral support came from contested areas outside the North and South West regions. This ultimately allowed the PAP to maintain its critical 60% baseline across the western half of the country.

#### Sample count accuracies

- Vote counts below are for votes cast in Singapore only and exclude votes cast overseas. While most constituencies had accuracies that fall within the 4% [margin of error](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margin_of_error "Margin of error") (at a 95% [confidence interval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confidence_interval "Confidence interval")), there are only three constituencies that had accuracies off above 4%, which are indicated in red text.
